So yeah, two years and three months exactly since that festival job.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, or has that also been affected?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really don't. It's like... it's like all the color has been drained out of everything. Even when I try, you know? Like, a few weeks ago, my friend Sarah—she's the one who usually brings the spinach dip to game night, she makes it from scratch with the water chestnuts and everything—she practically dragged me to a movie. It was a comedy, one of those big summer blockbusters that everyone was talking about, and I used to love those. I'd get the giant bucket of popcorn with the layered butter and the big blue slushie, and I'd just laugh until my sides hurt. But sitting there in the theater with Sarah, I was just... blank. Everyone else was laughing, the whole theater was just roaring, and I was just staring at the screen thinking about how sticky the floor was and how loud the speakers were. I didn't feel a single ounce of joy or amusement. I just wanted to go home and get back in bed. And it's the same with the cooking. I tried to make this simple chicken piccata last month, thinking maybe if I just forced myself to do it, the enjoyment would come back. I bought the capers, the lemons, the thin-sliced chicken breasts. I even poured myself a glass of white wine, which I always used to do when I cooked. But standing there at the stove, squeezing the lemon juice, I just felt nothing. It didn't smell good to me, I didn't care how it tasted, and I ended up throwing half of it away because I just couldn't even be bothered to eat it. It's just all gone. The fun, the pleasure, the satisfaction... it's all just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... honestly, all the time. It's like this loop playing in my head that I just can't turn off, no matter how hard I try. I just... I keep telling myself I should have known better. Like, why did I even take that booking at the festival in the first place? I didn't need to be there. I could have just stayed home or picked up a different gig, you know? There were other options. I remember looking at the schedule and thinking, 'Oh, the festival will be fun, it'll be a nice change of pace.' But I should have known. I should have trusted my gut or... or just been more careful. And then, when it actually happened, when the robbery was going down... I just keep thinking about the people who were there helping me, the other temporary staff who were just trying to do their jobs, just like me. We were all just trying to get through the day, handing out wristbands and answering questions about where the bathrooms were. And I just... I feel like I completely failed them. I was supposed to be, I don't know, looking out for things, keeping an eye on the cash box, making sure everything was secure. And I didn't keep them safe. I didn't protect them from what happened. I just stood there, and now they have to live with that memory too, and it's because I wasn't good enough or smart enough to stop it or see it coming. I just carry all this guilt around every single day, like a physical weight, just constantly blaming myself for every little decision I made that led up to that moment. If I had just said no to the job, none of us would have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I know I just said how empty everything feels, and how I don't care about the cooking or the hiking or any of it anymore, and how heavy the guilt is. It really is heavy, like I physically feel it in my shoulders when I wake up in the morning. Sometimes I just lie there looking at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades, and I think about how exhausting it is to just get through another day feeling like this. But... no, I've never actually wanted to die. I don't want to hurt myself. It's more like I just want the feelings to stop, you know? I want the loop in my head to turn off. I want to be able to go to a movie with Sarah and actually taste the popcorn and laugh at the jokes instead of just existing in this weird, gray bubble. But I don't want to end it all. I just... I just want to go back to how I was before that festival. I want my old self back, not to be dead. So, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that, even on the days when I can't bring myself to leave the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's really constant. It's not just a passing feeling, it's like this permanent fixture in my life now. Most days, there is just this low, sour mix of fear and guilt sitting right under everything I do. Even when nothing bad is actually going on, you know? Like yesterday, I was just sitting in my living room. The sun was shining through the window, it was a perfectly normal Tuesday afternoon. The mail carrier had already come by—I heard the squeak of the mailbox lid, which usually doesn't bother me—and I was just trying to fold a basket of laundry. Just simple things, right? Towels, some t-shirts, nothing complicated. But my heart was just racing, and my stomach felt like it was tied in these tight, awful knots. \n\nIt's like my body is just waiting for the other shoe to drop, even when I'm completely safe in my own house with the doors locked. I'll be matching socks, and suddenly I'm feeling this overwhelming sense of dread, like I've done something terribly wrong or something awful is about to happen to me right that second. And the guilt is always there, too, mixed right in with the fear. It's like a bad taste in the back of my mouth that I can't wash out, no matter how much water I drink or how many times I brush my teeth. I'll be sitting there, perfectly safe, and I just feel so keyed up and so... so heavy with it all. It doesn't matter if I'm watching TV or trying to read a magazine—which I can't even focus on anyway, I just read the same paragraph over and over—that nervous, on-edge feeling is just always humming in the background, like a refrigerator that never turns off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending more time alone since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not that I completely shut everyone out, though I guess I do spend a lot more time by myself in the house now. I still answer my friends sometimes, you know? Like, if they text me to check in. Sarah texted me the other day to see if I wanted to grab coffee at this little diner down the street, the one with the red vinyl booths we always used to go to. And I did text her back, eventually, and I even forced myself to go. \n\nBut... it's just so hard now. Because even when I am with them, sitting right across the table with a cup of coffee right in front of me, it doesn't feel the same. I just feel like I am watching from far away. Like I'm floating somewhere up near the ceiling, just observing these two people having a conversation. She was talking about her week, and I was nodding and making all the right facial expressions, but I wasn't really there. \n\nI just... I do not know how to jump back into being close like before. We used to be able to talk for hours, just finishing each other's sentences and laughing over the silliest things, and now I sit there and it's like there's this thick pane of glass between us. I want to reach through it, I want to feel connected to her again, but I just can't figure out how to do it anymore. I don't know how to bridge that gap. So even when I'm not physically alone, I still end up feeling completely isolated, which honestly just makes it feel easier to stay home in the first place, rather than going out and having to pretend I'm really present.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any big changes, anyway. I mean, my clothes still fit the same as they always have. Like, I have this pair of jeans, these dark blue ones that I always used to wear when we'd go out on the weekends, and they still button just fine. I wore them yesterday, actually, when I was just sitting around the house trying to figure out what to do with myself. \n\nAnd I guess I'm still eating about the same amount, though I really don't care about the food at all anymore. Like I said before, I used to love cooking. I'd spend hours on a Sunday trying out a new recipe, chopping vegetables, getting the spices just right, making a whole mess in the kitchen. Now, I just... I don't know, I just eat because I know I'm supposed to. I'll make a sandwich, just turkey and cheese on that wheat bread from the corner store, or I'll heat up some soup from a can. It's just fuel now. There's no joy in it. I don't even really taste it, to be honest. It's just something I do so my stomach stops growling. \n\nBut as far as my actual weight goes? No, I haven't stepped on a scale recently or anything, but I haven't noticed any difference in how I look or how my clothes feel. I guess my body is just staying exactly the same, even though everything inside my head feels completely different. It's weird, isn't it? How your body just keeps going, just keeps maintaining its weight and doing its normal things, while your brain feels like it's stuck in this awful, exhausting loop. But yeah, to answer your question, no changes in my weight that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I'm hallucinating or going crazy, like seeing ghosts or hearing people talking to me from the walls or anything like that. But... there are these moments. These really awful, intense moments where it feels like I'm right back there. \n\nLike, I used to work at this little bakery, right? And they had this old metal cash box they kept under the counter for big bills. It had this very specific, sharp *snap* when you closed it. And now... if I'm at a store, or even just somewhere and I hear a sound like that—a metal drawer slamming, or a latch snapping shut—or if I'm just standing in my own kitchen making that turkey sandwich I told you about, and someone, like my husband, walks up behind me unexpectedly... it just all comes rushing back. \n\nIt's not just a memory, it's... it's so fast and so real. I can literally hear the voices from the robbery again, just as loud and as terrifying as they were that day. I hear the shouting, the exact words they were screaming. And my stomach just completely drops out from under me, like I'm on a rollercoaster going over the edge. My heart pounds, and for a split second, my brain really, truly believes it is happening right then and there all over again. It's like I'm transported back to that exact second, and I have to look around and blink and remind myself that I'm in my house, or at the grocery store, and not back behind that counter. So, I guess I do hear things, but it's just... it's the sounds from that day, playing on a loop in my head whenever something startles me. It takes me so long to calm down after that happens, to convince my body that the voices aren't real anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like out of nowhere, I don't think. I mean, I don't just sit there on the couch watching TV and suddenly feel terrified for absolutely no reason at all. It's not like a switch flips in an empty room, you know? \n\nIt's more about what I was just saying, about the sounds and being startled. Like, if I'm just sitting there and it's quiet, I'm okay. Or, well, as okay as I get these days. But if I hear that metal snapping sound, or if someone drops a pan in the kitchen and it clatters, or if my husband comes up behind me when I'm at the sink and I don't hear him coming... that's when the fear hits. And it's intense, like I said, my heart just races and my stomach drops and I feel like I can't breathe, but it's always because something triggered it. It's always tied to a noise or a surprise that reminds me of... of that day at the bakery. \n\nSo it's not just this random wave of panic that washes over me while I'm reading a book or something. There's always a reason, even if it's a stupid reason like a car backfiring down the street. It's like my body is constantly on high alert, waiting for the next bad thing to happen, and the second it thinks it hears it, boom, I'm terrified. But it's not... it doesn't just happen on its own, if that makes sense. It needs that spark to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering the robbery at the festival, even when you don't want to think about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, but... wait, festival? No, it wasn't a festival. I was working at a bakery. I don't know why you said festival. But anyway, to answer your question about remembering it when I don't want to... yes, absolutely. All the time. But it's exactly what I was trying to explain a minute ago. It's not like I'm just sitting around daydreaming and then suddenly decide to think about it. It's those triggers. \n\nLike I was saying about the cash box? If I hear a sound like that—a sharp, metal snapping sound—or if I'm just standing in my kitchen, minding my own business, maybe making a sandwich or doing the dishes, and somebody, like my husband, comes up behind me and I don't expect it... it just brings the whole robbery rushing back. And it happens so incredibly fast. One second I'm rinsing a plate, and the next second, I can literally hear the voices of those men again, just screaming and yelling, exactly the way they did that day. \n\nAnd it's not just in my head, my whole body reacts. My stomach drops, just completely falls out from under me, like I'm on a rollercoaster or something. It feels exactly like it is happening right then, in that very moment, all over again. I don't want to think about it, I really don't. I try so hard to just focus on normal things, like the dishes or the TV, but those sounds or being startled... they just rip me right back to the bakery. It's exhausting, honestly. Just completely exhausting to feel like you're constantly fighting off a memory that's trying to swallow you whole.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Well, sleep... sleep is actually okay, I guess. I mean, I don't really have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep, not that I've noticed anyway. It's not like I'm lying awake staring at the ceiling for hours, tossing and turning, worrying about the bakery or anything like that. My body is just done. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble sleeping. I usually get my solid seven or eight hours. It's really just the daytime that's the problem. The daytime is when all the... the triggers happen, when I hear those sounds and my stomach drops and I feel like I'm right back behind that counter. But at night, once I'm actually in bed and it's quiet and dark, I seem to be able to just shut down and sleep. I guess I'm thankful for that, at least. If I couldn't sleep on top of everything else, I don't know how I'd function at all. But yeah, sleep hasn't really been an issue for me since all this started.

Now, when they text me about it, I just stare at my phone and try to think of an excuse not to go. It all just feels like... like obligations I keep putting off, instead of things I actually want to do. I just barely care about any of it anymore, and I don't know why.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like things have become pointless?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I know exactly when it started, actually, because it's tied to this specific thing that happened. I was working this job at a festival—just, you know, one of those temporary gigs where you're helping out with the crowds and the vendors and all that. It wasn't a fancy career or anything, just something to get by at the time. But anyway, there was a robbery while I was working there. \n\nAnd that was... gosh, I've kept track of it so closely in my head... it was about two years and three months ago now. I remember because it was right around the time the weather was starting to change, and you know how you kind of associate certain feelings with the time of year? Well, ever since that day at the festival, ever since that robbery, it's just been like a switch flipped. \n\nBefore that, I was totally fine, doing my dance routines in the living room and hiking all over the place, like I was telling you. But for the last two years and three months, it's just been this big, long stretch of... I don't know, just feeling completely empty about all of it. I keep thinking, oh, maybe next month I'll want to go to game night again, or maybe I'll finally want to cook a real meal, but it's been over two years now of just staring at my boots by the door and not caring. So yeah, two years and three months exactly since that festival job.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, or has that also been affected?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really don't. It's like... it's like all the color has been drained out of everything. Even when I try, you know? Like, a few weeks ago, my friend Sarah—she's the one who usually brings the spinach dip to game night, she makes it from scratch with the water chestnuts and everything—she practically dragged me to a movie. It was a comedy, one of those big summer blockbusters that everyone was talking about, and I used to love those. I'd get the giant bucket of popcorn with the layered butter and the big blue slushie, and I'd just laugh until my sides hurt. But sitting there in the theater with Sarah, I was just... blank. Everyone else was laughing, the whole theater was just roaring, and I was just staring at the screen thinking about how sticky the floor was and how loud the speakers were. I didn't feel a single ounce of joy or amusement. I just wanted to go home and get back in bed. And it's the same with the cooking. I tried to make this simple chicken piccata last month, thinking maybe if I just forced myself to do it, the enjoyment would come back. I bought the capers, the lemons, the thin-sliced chicken breasts. I even poured myself a glass of white wine, which I always used to do when I cooked. But standing there at the stove, squeezing the lemon juice, I just felt nothing. It didn't smell good to me, I didn't care how it tasted, and I ended up throwing half of it away because I just couldn't even be bothered to eat it. It's just all gone. The fun, the pleasure, the satisfaction... it's all just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... honestly, all the time. It's like this loop playing in my head that I just can't turn off, no matter how hard I try. I just... I keep telling myself I should have known better. Like, why did I even take that booking at the festival in the first place? I didn't need to be there. I could have just stayed home or picked up a different gig, you know? There were other options. I remember looking at the schedule and thinking, 'Oh, the festival will be fun, it'll be a nice change of pace.' But I should have known. I should have trusted my gut or... or just been more careful. And then, when it actually happened, when the robbery was going down... I just keep thinking about the people who were there helping me, the other temporary staff who were just trying to do their jobs, just like me. We were all just trying to get through the day, handing out wristbands and answering questions about where the bathrooms were. And I just... I feel like I completely failed them. I was supposed to be, I don't know, looking out for things, keeping an eye on the cash box, making sure everything was secure. And I didn't keep them safe. I didn't protect them from what happened. I just stood there, and now they have to live with that memory too, and it's because I wasn't good enough or smart enough to stop it or see it coming. I just carry all this guilt around every single day, like a physical weight, just constantly blaming myself for every little decision I made that led up to that moment. If I had just said no to the job, none of us would have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I know I just said how empty everything feels, and how I don't care about the cooking or the hiking or any of it anymore, and how heavy the guilt is. It really is heavy, like I physically feel it in my shoulders when I wake up in the morning. Sometimes I just lie there looking at the ceiling fan, watching the dust on the blades, and I think about how exhausting it is to just get through another day feeling like this. But... no, I've never actually wanted to die. I don't want to hurt myself. It's more like I just want the feelings to stop, you know? I want the loop in my head to turn off. I want to be able to go to a movie with Sarah and actually taste the popcorn and laugh at the jokes instead of just existing in this weird, gray bubble. But I don't want to end it all. I just... I just want to go back to how I was before that festival. I want my old self back, not to be dead. So, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that, even on the days when I can't bring myself to leave the house.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's really constant. It's not just a passing feeling, it's like this permanent fixture in my life now. Most days, there is just this low, sour mix of fear and guilt sitting right under everything I do. Even when nothing bad is actually going on, you know? Like yesterday, I was just sitting in my living room. The sun was shining through the window, it was a perfectly normal Tuesday afternoon. The mail carrier had already come by—I heard the squeak of the mailbox lid, which usually doesn't bother me—and I was just trying to fold a basket of laundry. Just simple things, right? Towels, some t-shirts, nothing complicated. But my heart was just racing, and my stomach felt like it was tied in these tight, awful knots. \n\nIt's like my body is just waiting for the other shoe to drop, even when I'm completely safe in my own house with the doors locked. I'll be matching socks, and suddenly I'm feeling this overwhelming sense of dread, like I've done something terribly wrong or something awful is about to happen to me right that second. And the guilt is always there, too, mixed right in with the fear. It's like a bad taste in the back of my mouth that I can't wash out, no matter how much water I drink or how many times I brush my teeth. I'll be sitting there, perfectly safe, and I just feel so keyed up and so... so heavy with it all. It doesn't matter if I'm watching TV or trying to read a magazine—which I can't even focus on anyway, I just read the same paragraph over and over—that nervous, on-edge feeling is just always humming in the background, like a refrigerator that never turns off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending more time alone since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not that I completely shut everyone out, though I guess I do spend a lot more time by myself in the house now. I still answer my friends sometimes, you know? Like, if they text me to check in. Sarah texted me the other day to see if I wanted to grab coffee at this little diner down the street, the one with the red vinyl booths we always used to go to. And I did text her back, eventually, and I even forced myself to go. \n\nBut... it's just so hard now. Because even when I am with them, sitting right across the table with a cup of coffee right in front of me, it doesn't feel the same. I just feel like I am watching from far away. Like I'm floating somewhere up near the ceiling, just observing these two people having a conversation. She was talking about her week, and I was nodding and making all the right facial expressions, but I wasn't really there. \n\nI just... I do not know how to jump back into being close like before. We used to be able to talk for hours, just finishing each other's sentences and laughing over the silliest things, and now I sit there and it's like there's this thick pane of glass between us. I want to reach through it, I want to feel connected to her again, but I just can't figure out how to do it anymore. I don't know how to bridge that gap. So even when I'm not physically alone, I still end up feeling completely isolated, which honestly just makes it feel easier to stay home in the first place, rather than going out and having to pretend I'm really present.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any big changes, anyway. I mean, my clothes still fit the same as they always have. Like, I have this pair of jeans, these dark blue ones that I always used to wear when we'd go out on the weekends, and they still button just fine. I wore them yesterday, actually, when I was just sitting around the house trying to figure out what to do with myself. \n\nAnd I guess I'm still eating about the same amount, though I really don't care about the food at all anymore. Like I said before, I used to love cooking. I'd spend hours on a Sunday trying out a new recipe, chopping vegetables, getting the spices just right, making a whole mess in the kitchen. Now, I just... I don't know, I just eat because I know I'm supposed to. I'll make a sandwich, just turkey and cheese on that wheat bread from the corner store, or I'll heat up some soup from a can. It's just fuel now. There's no joy in it. I don't even really taste it, to be honest. It's just something I do so my stomach stops growling. \n\nBut as far as my actual weight goes? No, I haven't stepped on a scale recently or anything, but I haven't noticed any difference in how I look or how my clothes feel. I guess my body is just staying exactly the same, even though everything inside my head feels completely different. It's weird, isn't it? How your body just keeps going, just keeps maintaining its weight and doing its normal things, while your brain feels like it's stuck in this awful, exhausting loop. But yeah, to answer your question, no changes in my weight that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that weren't really there since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not like I'm hallucinating or going crazy, like seeing ghosts or hearing people talking to me from the walls or anything like that. But... there are these moments. These really awful, intense moments where it feels like I'm right back there. \n\nLike, I used to work at this little bakery, right? And they had this old metal cash box they kept under the counter for big bills. It had this very specific, sharp *snap* when you closed it. And now... if I'm at a store, or even just somewhere and I hear a sound like that—a metal drawer slamming, or a latch snapping shut—or if I'm just standing in my own kitchen making that turkey sandwich I told you about, and someone, like my husband, walks up behind me unexpectedly... it just all comes rushing back. \n\nIt's not just a memory, it's... it's so fast and so real. I can literally hear the voices from the robbery again, just as loud and as terrifying as they were that day. I hear the shouting, the exact words they were screaming. And my stomach just completely drops out from under me, like I'm on a rollercoaster going over the edge. My heart pounds, and for a split second, my brain really, truly believes it is happening right then and there all over again. It's like I'm transported back to that exact second, and I have to look around and blink and remind myself that I'm in my house, or at the grocery store, and not back behind that counter. So, I guess I do hear things, but it's just... it's the sounds from that day, playing on a loop in my head whenever something startles me. It takes me so long to calm down after that happens, to convince my body that the voices aren't real anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like out of nowhere, I don't think. I mean, I don't just sit there on the couch watching TV and suddenly feel terrified for absolutely no reason at all. It's not like a switch flips in an empty room, you know? \n\nIt's more about what I was just saying, about the sounds and being startled. Like, if I'm just sitting there and it's quiet, I'm okay. Or, well, as okay as I get these days. But if I hear that metal snapping sound, or if someone drops a pan in the kitchen and it clatters, or if my husband comes up behind me when I'm at the sink and I don't hear him coming... that's when the fear hits. And it's intense, like I said, my heart just races and my stomach drops and I feel like I can't breathe, but it's always because something triggered it. It's always tied to a noise or a surprise that reminds me of... of that day at the bakery. \n\nSo it's not just this random wave of panic that washes over me while I'm reading a book or something. There's always a reason, even if it's a stupid reason like a car backfiring down the street. It's like my body is constantly on high alert, waiting for the next bad thing to happen, and the second it thinks it hears it, boom, I'm terrified. But it's not... it doesn't just happen on its own, if that makes sense. It needs that spark to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself remembering the robbery at the festival, even when you don't want to think about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, but... wait, festival? No, it wasn't a festival. I was working at a bakery. I don't know why you said festival. But anyway, to answer your question about remembering it when I don't want to... yes, absolutely. All the time. But it's exactly what I was trying to explain a minute ago. It's not like I'm just sitting around daydreaming and then suddenly decide to think about it. It's those triggers. \n\nLike I was saying about the cash box? If I hear a sound like that—a sharp, metal snapping sound—or if I'm just standing in my kitchen, minding my own business, maybe making a sandwich or doing the dishes, and somebody, like my husband, comes up behind me and I don't expect it... it just brings the whole robbery rushing back. And it happens so incredibly fast. One second I'm rinsing a plate, and the next second, I can literally hear the voices of those men again, just screaming and yelling, exactly the way they did that day. \n\nAnd it's not just in my head, my whole body reacts. My stomach drops, just completely falls out from under me, like I'm on a rollercoaster or something. It feels exactly like it is happening right then, in that very moment, all over again. I don't want to think about it, I really don't. I try so hard to just focus on normal things, like the dishes or the TV, but those sounds or being startled... they just rip me right back to the bakery. It's exhausting, honestly. Just completely exhausting to feel like you're constantly fighting off a memory that's trying to swallow you whole.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is actually okay, I guess. I mean, I don't really have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep, not that I've noticed anyway. It's not like I'm lying awake staring at the ceiling for hours, tossing and turning, worrying about the bakery or anything like that. When I go to bed, I'm usually just so incredibly tired from the day—you know, from being on high alert all the time, jumping at every little sound, like I was telling you about with the metal snapping noises and my husband walking up behind me—that by the time my head hits the pillow, I'm just exhausted. My body is just done. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble sleeping. I usually get my solid seven or eight hours. It's really just the daytime that's the problem. The daytime is when all the... the triggers happen, when I hear those sounds and my stomach drops and I feel like I'm right back behind that counter. But at night, once I'm actually in bed and it's quiet and dark, I seem to be able to just shut down and sleep. I guess I'm thankful for that, at least. If I couldn't sleep on top of everything else, I don't know how I'd function at all. But yeah, sleep hasn't really been an issue for me since all this started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F43.1"], "note": "The patient's symptoms, such as persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the trauma, negative alterations in cognition and mood, and marked arousal and reactivity, are consistent with post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), which is coded as F43.1 in the ICD-10. Previous candidates related to depressive episodes (F32) and recurrent depressive disorder (F33) have been ruled out due to the patient's specific symptoms and triggers related to the traumatic event.", "is_final": true}
